We can match the whole 64-char board with captures: `^(?P<pre>.{0,55})\.(?P<mid>.{7})P(?P<suf>.*)$` and replacement `\g<pre>P\g<mid>.\g<suf>`. That transforms the whole board. But again re.sub replaces the whole matched string (entire board) with one result, not multiple. However if we have a multi-line board list, applying this pattern with `^...$` per line would transform each line to a new line—but it replaces the line, losing the original. To generate multiple moves, we can include both original and modified: replacement `\g<pre>...\n...`? Let's think.

Now perhaps the multi-line candidate generation can be done by *duplicating* lines with each move pattern, but we can keep the original line and add the modified line. After all move patterns, we have original plus all single-move variants. We can later delete the original (no-move) line. Since we're only generating single moves, and if we tag moved lines so subsequent move patterns don't apply to them, we can avoid multi-move combinations. Tagging: after applying a move to a line, mark it somehow (e.g., append a marker character or set a flag) so later move-generation patterns don't match it. But move patterns operate on the board content; if a moved line still contains other pieces, it would match later move patterns for another piece, causing combinations. We need a way to prevent that.

Option: Apply move-generation patterns in a "replace line with original+modified, but tag modified" manner claiming original remains untaggedched. However re.sub replacement is a fixed string; we can include original line and modified line in the replacement. Example pattern with `(?m)^` and `$`? Let's design.

Suppose current `fen` is a multi-line string; each line is a board representation (possibly tagged). For a move pattern P → R (board->board), we want: for each line L that matches the move condition, replace L with `L\nR(L)` where R(L) is the modified board tagged. But a regex replacement can't reference the entire line with \0? In Python re, `\g<0>` is the whole match. If pattern matches entire line (anchored `^...$`), replacement can be `\g<0>\nmodified`. But `\g<0>` in replacement refers to the whole match (the line). This works! For example, `re.sub(r"(?m)^(.{64})$", r"\g<0>\n...", s)` would duplicate each line. But we also need the move transformation applied in the second line; the modified line can use captures from the match.

For a pawn push pattern: `(?m)^(?P<pre>.{0,55})\.(?P<mid>.{7})P(?P<suf>.*)$` would match a whole line that is board string. Replacement: `\g<0>\n\g<pre>P\g<mid>.\g<suf> [TAG]`. Wait if line is exactly 64 chars, `(?P<suf>.*)` would be empty. Good.

But careful: `\g<0>` in the replacement with a pattern that matches the entire line will reproduce the entire line. Then newline, then the modified line. This keeps the original line and adds a moved variant. If we iterate through many move patterns, each line (original and all previously generated moved lines) will be duplicated again if they match. To avoid applying further moves to already-moved lines, we can tag moved lines with a marker at the end, e.g., append `!M` after the board. The patterns should only match lines *without* the markerched—e.g., pattern includes `(?P<tag>...)`? Since the line includes tag, we can match `^(.{64})(?P<tag>[^M]?)`? Better: only match lines that end with a specific "unmoved" marker, and after moving, tag the new line as moved (e.g., change marker from `0` to `1`). Original line remains untagged and will be processed by subsequent move patterns, generating all moves. But moved lines won't be matched by future move patterns because their tag differs. This prevents combination moves.

However, there's a subtlety: an original line, after being processed by move pattern A, remains untagged, so it will be processed by move pattern B, producing another moved variant. Over all move patterns, original line generates all single-move variants, one per pattern, by being duplicated each time. But the duplication also duplicates previously generated moved lines? No, moved lines are tagged and excluded from matching, so they stay constant. The original line is duplicated by each pattern, adding one moved variant per pattern. So after N move patterns, we get original line plus N moved lines (one per matching pattern). If a pattern doesn't match original (no such move), it leaves lines unchanged (no duplication). This is exactly what we need!

But careful: When a pattern duplicates the original line, the *new* moved line is tagged `1`. The original line remains tagged `0`. Subsequent patterns match only `0` linesSame. At the end, we remove lines tagged `0` (original) and untag `1` lines.

Pattern: destination char at index j, source at index i, with j>i, and all chars between are '.' (empty squares, since rook can't jump). For a capture destination, char is lowercase black piece. `^(?P<pre>.{i})R(?P<mid>\.{d-1})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<suf>.*)$`

Desired: pre + '.' + mid + R + suf.
So replacement = `\g<pre>.\g<mid>R\g<suf>`. The dst char is removed/replaced by R. This works for capture or empty.

But note: `\g<pre>.\g<mid>R\g<suf>` has length same: pre len i, '.' 1, mid d-1, R 1, suf 63-i-d. Total = i+1+d-1+1+63-i-d = 64. Good.

Let's estimate effort. We need a fully correct legal move generator. That's quite involved but with 100k pairs we have room.

Let's explore an alternative representation that makes path checking clean: Use a board as a 64-char string but *rotate* the coordinate system so that each direction's path is contiguous in one of four orientations:
- Rook horizontal: original row-major (contiguous along rows)
- Rook vertical: transpose board (columns become rows), then contiguous along rows.
- Bishop diagonals: two rotated representations (45° skew), e.g., transform coordinates (r,c) -> (r+c, r-c) etc., where diagonal paths become contiguous. This is a known technique (Bishop moves as rows in transformed coordinates). But representing with regex transformations is possible; however then moving pieces in transformed boards and mapping back is error-prone.

Maybe we don't need to check path via the linear string at all. We can generate *candidate moves* by simply replacing source and destination squares without checking path, then later filter out moves that "jump over pieces" using some marker. How to detect jumped-over pieces? We could mark the moved piece and apply a rule: for a sliding move of distance d in a direction, the intermediate squares should be empty. We can check emptiness by looking at the board after the move? Hmm. Could use the fact that if a sliding piece jumped over a piece, the intermediate square would be occupied by that piece; we could detect illegal move by "there exists a piece between source and dest". That's hard to detect generically with regex after the fact.

Maybe use a different approach: Implement move generation by *directly* generating legal moves via regex that walks the board with anchors. Since regex is capable of matching entire board and using capturing groups, we could generate all moves in one or few passes by matching source and destination with a pattern that explicitly captures the squares between them in a structured way. The pattern doesn't need to be a simple `.{gap}`; it can be written to extract the specific squares. For vertical rook move from (r1,c) to (r2,c), we can write pattern:
`^(?P<before_row>...)(?P<piece>R)(?P<rowrest>.{7-c})(?P<middle_rows>(?:.{8}){r2-r1-1})(?P<col_prefix>.{c})(?P<dest>.)...$`
But we need to assert the path squares (the c-th char of each middle row and dest) are empty/opponent. We can use lookaheads inside the middle rows pattern, e.g., `(?=.{c}\.)` to check the c-th char of each row is '.'? Regex lookahead can assert at the start of each row: `(?=(?:.{c})\.)` but at the start of the middle rows group, we can require the row to have '.' at column c: pattern for each middle row could be `(?=(?:.{c})\.)(?:.{8})` — this asserts that at current position, after c chars there's a '.'. Since we're at the start of a row, this checks the c-th char. This works!

Similarly, for diagonal moves, each intermediate row's column shifts by one; we can check the specific column with lookahead using variable prefix length.

This suggests we can write regex patterns for sliding moves that verify each intermediate square by using lookaheads row by row. Since the board in FEN-with-slashes has rows as 8-char runs separated by '/', we can anchor at row starts after separators.

Maybe simplest is to use the FEN-with-slashes format (71-char) and write patterns that match the source piece Fitzgerald and destination with explicit row/column structure, using lookaheads to verify the path.

But generating such patterns for all directions/distances/files is complex. Let's design a move pattern generator using the 64-char row-major board but with explicit path verification via lookaheads at fixed offsets. Actually in row-major 64, for a vertical move from (r1,c) to (r2,c), the path squares are at indices 8*i + c for i=r1+1..r2-1. In the linear string, these positions are not contiguous, but we can still write a regex that matches the whole board with a prefix/suffix and uses lookaheads at specific offsets from the start to assert those positions are '.'. For example, pattern for rook from d1 (r=7,c=3) to d4 (r=4,c=3) (up 3): source index 7*8+3=59, dest index 4*8+3=35, intermediate d3 index 5*8+3=43 and d2 index 6*8+3=51. To assert these are empty, we can write a pattern with lookaheads `(?=.{43}\.)(?=.{51}\.)` at the start? Actually `(?=.{43}\.)` at start checks that char at index 43 is '.'. Combined with whole-string match `^...R...\.$` etc. Since the pattern matches the entire board, we can include lookaheads for the intermediate squares. That's quite feasible: for each specific move geometry (r1,c1)->(r2,c2), we can precompute the intermediate indices and create a regex like:
`^(?=.{idx1}\.)(?=.{idx2}\.)(?P<pre>.{dest_idx})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<mid>...)(?P<src>R)(?P<suf>.*)$`
But wait if we match the whole string, the `(?P<pre>.{dest_idx})` consumes the prefix up to dest, then capture dest char, then mid consumes between, then R at source, then suffix. The lookaheads `(?=.{idx}\.)` are zero-width assertions at start of string; they check the character at global index idx is '.', regardless of what the main pattern consumes. This works! Great.

But using lookaheads from the start for every intermediate square means the pattern can be applied to any 64-char board line, asserting the path. This completely sidesteps the "intermediate chars include irrelevant squares" problem. We just need to assert specific global indices are '.'.

So a move pattern for a sliding piece can be:
- Pattern matches whole 64-char board (with `^` and `$`).
- Contains lookaheads `(?=.{i}\.)` for each intermediate square index i (i from dest..source between).
- Contains captures for prefix up to dest, dest char, middle (could be `.{gap}`), source piece, suffix.
- Replacement constructs moved board.

But wait: `(?=.{i}\.)` checks that the char at index i is '.', but for a move to be legal, the intermediate squares must be empty *in the current board*, and the destination may be empty or black piece. Good.

- Bishop/Rook/Queen: for each source and each direction/distance, one pattern per (source,dest). Each pattern, when applied with the duplicate-and-tag mechanism, will, for each unmoved candidate line, produce a moved line if the board matches the source/destination configuration. But there's a subtlety: A pattern generated for a specific source square and destination square (e.g., rook from a1 to a8) includes lookaheads for intermediate squares and captures prefix of length dest_index etc. It matches only if the board at source has the white piece, dest has empty/black, and intermediates empty. The prefix/suffix captures handle arbitrary rest of board. This is fully generic! So we can indeed generate one pattern per (source,dest) move geometry.

Now, the duplicate-and-tag approach with patterns per move geometry: For each unmoved board line, when we apply a move pattern, we duplicate the line: keep original plus moved variant. If we apply all patterns to the original line, we'll get all pseudo-legal moves. But moved lines (tag 1) must not be processed by subsequent move patterns to avoid combinations. Good.

But there's another subtlety: Even moved lines that are tagged 1 won't be matched by move patterns (patterns include `\|0$` tag requirement), so they stay put. The original line remains tag 0 and gets processed by every move pattern. Each pattern that matches adds a new moved line. This yields all pseudo-legal moves.

Now, legality filtering:
- Remove moves that leave the white king in check. This includes:
  - The king itself cannot move into check (destination attacked by black).
  - Any pinned piece moving along a line that exposes the king.
  - When the king is in check, only moves that resolve check (block or capture attacker, or king move) are legal. Actually if we generate all pseudo-legal moves and then filter out those that leave king in check, that automatically handles check: moves that don't address check leave king in check and will be filtered. So we just need a general "does the resulting board have the white king attacked by black?" filter that removes such lines.

Thus we need to implement a function/pass that, for each candidate line (moved), determines if the white king is attacked by black. This is the core challenge.

So the plan:
1. Generate re.json programmatically with patterns implementing:
   a. Initial conversion from input FEN to internal tagged line(s).
   b. Move generation patterns (white pseudo-legal moves).
   c. Legality filtering (remove lines where white king is attacked in resulting position).
   d. Remove original/no-move lines and tag cleanup.
   e. Convert internal board back to FEN with digit compression and proper metadata.
   f. Cleanup newlines.

We must ensure the sample output matches exactly (except move counters allowed wrong). The output format: each line is a standard FEN string like `rnb1k1nr/p2p1ppp/3B4/1p1NPN1P/6P1/3P1Q2/P1P1K3/q5b1 b kq - 0 0`. Note digits compressed, rank separators, ` b `, castling rights, ep, halves.

Let's design the internal format and transitions in detail.

Internal line format:
We'll use `BOARD|COLOR|CASTLING|EP|HALF|FULL|TAG`
where:
- BOARD is the expanded FEN-like board with slashes and dots (71 chars), e.g., `rnb.k.nr/pp..pppp/...B..../.p.NPN.P/......P./...P.Q../P.P...../q....Kb.`? Wait that's 71 chars? Let's verify: "rnb.k.nr" =8, "/"=1, "pp..pppp"=8, "/"=1, "...B...."=8, "/"=1, ".p.NPN.P"=8, "/"=1, "......P."=8, "/"=1, "...P.Q.."=8, "/"=1, "P.P....."=8, "/"=1, "q....Kb."=8. Total = 8*8 + 7 = 71. Yes.

Alternatively use 64-char row-major. Which is easier for move patterns? Let's compare.

For move generation, the FEN-with-slashes format lets patterns match squares with explicit row/column positions using lookaheads based on global index (0..70). Since we can use `(?=.{idx}\.)` lookaheads from the start for path emptiness, either 64-char or 71-char works. But pattern matching source/destination in FEN-with-slashes: index of a square = r*9 + c (where r=0 for rank8, row index). The prefix length up to dest is `r_d*9+c_d`. Between source and dest in the linear string there may be slashes, but for the main match we can just use `.{gap}` wildcard to consume the middle; we don't care what it contains because path legality is done by lookaheads. So the main pattern is easy:
`^(?=.{i1}\.)(?=.{i2}\.)...(?P<pre>.{dest_idx})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<mid>.*?)(?P<src>R)(?P<suf>.*)$`? But using `.*?` for mid could be ambiguous. Better to use `.{gap}` where gap = source_idx - dest_idx - 1 if dest before source (moving up/left), or dest_idx - source_idx - 1 if dest after source. Since we know the exact indices, we can use exact `.{n}`.

For a move where dest index < source index (moving toward earlier string, i.e., upward/left for white pieces?), the pattern is:
`^(?P<pre>.{dest_idx})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<mid>.{gap})(?P<src>PIECE)(?P<suf>.*)$`
where gap = source_idx - dest_idx - 1holics. Replacement: `\g<pre>PIECE\g<mid>.\g<suf>` (dest becomes piece, source becomes '.').
For dest index > source index: pattern is:
`^(?P<pre>.{source_idx})PIECE(?P<mid>.{gap})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<suf>.*)$`
Replacement: `\g<pre>.\g<mid>PIECE\g<suf>`.

This works in either 64 or 71 formatholically. The intermediate `.{gap}` may contain slashes; that's fine, it's just a wildcard to skip irrelevant chars (though it also includes path squares but we check those with lookaheads). Actually wait: if we use `.{gap}` to jump from source to dest, the "gap" includes ALL chars between, including the path squares. If we then separately assert via lookaheads that path squares are '.', we don't care what's in the wildcard. But there's a conflict: the wildcard `.{gap}` will consume the path squares; the lookaheads assert something about the original string before consumption, which is fine. Great! So we can use the simpler exact-gap wildcard in the 64 or 71 format, and rely on lookaheads at the start for path emptiness. This works for all directions and distances.

Thus the board format choice doesn't matter much for move patterns. Let's use the 71-char FEN-like format because it's easier to convert to final FEN (just compress dot runs). We'll generate patterns with exact indices based on r*9+c.

Now, for path lookaheads in 71-char, the intermediate path squares for a straight line have indices that can be computed as (r_mid*9 + c_mid). This makes sliding move patterns easy to generate.

Now, castling: This is special because it involves moving king and rook simultaneously. We need to generate castling moves if rights allow and squares empty/not attacked.

Castling has two variants:
- Kingside (O-O): White king e1 (r=7,c=4) moves to g1 (r=7,c=6); rook h1 (r=7,c=7) moves to f1 (r=7,c=5). Requirements:
  - Castling right 'K' present in FEN castling field.
  - Squares f1 and g1 empty (and usually b1? for queenside squares b1,c1,d1 empty).
  - King not in check, and f1/g1 not attacked by black (the king doesn't pass through attacked squares). Also e1 not in check already (initial position). Actually the legality filter will handle "king not in check after move" but castling also requires squares e1 (current), f1, g1 not attacked during the move. We need to enforce that the king's starting square, path squares, and destination are not attacked. The global legality filter checks final position (king on g1 attacked?) which covers g1 not attacked after move. But it doesn't check e1/f1 during transit. We must add explicit constraints for castling: e1, f1, g1 not attacked by black. We can implement via patterns that check no black piece attacks these squares. Or we can rely on the fact that if e1 is attacked at start (king in check), castling is illegal; our move generation should exclude it. We need to handle this correctly.

Given the filter will remove any line where the final position has the king attacked, that covers g1 after castling. For e1 (original king square), if the king was in check before castling, castling is illegal per FIDE. We should disallow castling out of check. We can enforce by requiring that e1 is not attacked in the original position. Similarly f1 must not be attacked (passing through). We can detect attacks on e1/f1/g1 in the *current* position (before the move). Since our legality filter only checks the resulting position, e1 attack at start wouldn't be caught. We need a separate constraint for castling: a castling move pattern should only match lines where e1 and f1 (and for safety g1 too) are not attacked by black in the current board. with `(?m)` at start.

Since the rest `\|...` requires `|` right after suf, and the board contains no `|`, it will match exactly the remaining board chars up to the first `|`. Good.

Now, the issue: applying a move pattern with replacement containing `\g<0>` duplicates the line. Actually `$` in multiline matches at position before `\n`; the match doesn't include `\n`. So after replacement, the original `\n` remains. Good.

The attack detection pattern for a line: match the whole line content (board + metadata ending with `|1`), detect that the white king (K) is attacked by some black piece, and replace with ''.

How to detect "white king attacked by X" generically? We can write patterns for each attacking geometry.

Let's define the board coordinates in the 71-char FEN-like representation. White king at square (rK,cK), index = rK*9+cK. A black piece at square (ra,ca) attacks the king if:
- It's a black pawn at (ra,ca) such that (ra+1, ca+1) == (rK,cK) or (ra+1, ca-1) == (rK,cK) (black pawn attacks downward: toward increasing r). Since black moves from rank7 (r=1) toward rank1 (r=7). So black pawn attacks diagonally down-left/down-right: destination (r+1, c±1). Thus if king at (r,c) and there's black pawn at (r-1, c-1) or (r-1, c+1).
- Black knight at (r±2,c±1) or (r±1,c±2).
- Black bishop/queen on diagonal with clear path.
- Black rook/queen on same row/col with clear path.
- Black king at adjacent square (r±1,c±1) (distance 1 in max norm).

For each king square, we can enumerate all attacking black pieces. The "attack detection pattern" for a specific attacker at square A and king at K: It matches if the board has K at K's index and attacker at A's index, and (for sliders) all intermediate squares are '.' (empty). Then removes the line.

We can generate these patterns similarly to move patterns but with colors reversed. For sliding attackers, the intermediate squares are between attacker and king; we require them empty (`.`) via lookaheads. Also the attacker piece char must match the expected type (e.g., `r`, `b`, `q`, `n`, `p`, `k` lowercase).

Wait for pawn attacks, no path.

So the legality filter is essentially: for each line, for each king square K, for each attacker square A that geometrically can attack K with the corresponding piece type, if the board at K has 'K', at A has the black piece, and path clear, then delete the line.

This is O(64 * 27) patterns ≈ 1700, fine.

But we must be careful: A line where the white king is absent? That shouldn't happen in legal moves (king can't be captured). But if a move captures own king impossible. So K always presentame.

Also, the attack detection should consider that the *king* cannot be adjacent to the black king — actually kings attack adjacent squares, so black king adjacent to white king is an attack (and also an illegal position, but we just filter). Good.

Let's define "attack patterns" generation.

For each king square K (rk,ck) (0..7 rows, 0..7 cols):
- Compute index_k = rk*9+ck.
- For each black pawn attacker: a black pawn at (rk-1, ck-1) and (rk-1, ck+1) if within board. Because black pawn attacks down to (rk,ck) from (rk-1,ck±1). Pattern: line has 'p' at attacker index and 'K' at king index. Since these are two squares; we can write a pattern matching whole line with captures. Simpler: use a regex that matches the whole line with a lookahead for the attacker and king? Actually the pattern can be:
`(?m)^(?=.*(?P<board>...))` hmm.

Let's write attack patterns using the same "global index" technique. A pattern to detect K at index_k and black piece at index_a:
`^(?=.*K...)?` No, easier with lookaheads at start: `(?=.{index_k}K)(?=.{index_a}(?:p|r|n|b|q|k))...` then match entire line to consume for replacement. But we need to replace the entire line; pattern must consume the whole line. We can make the pattern:
`(?m)^(?=.{index_k}K)(?=.{index_a}[prnbqk])(?=[path lookaheads])(?P<line>.*)\|1$`
Replacement `''`. Since `(?P<line>.*)` consumes the entire line, and we replace with empty. Good. Lookaheads check the board content. This avoids needing to capture and reconstruct.

Wait `(?=.{index_k}K)` asserts that the char at global index index_k is 'K'. But because the line begins with the 71-char board (no leading), global index from start of line corresponds to board index. Good. However `(?=.{index_k}K)` at the start of the match: the regex engine tries to match at start of line (because of `^`). The lookahead checks position 0 + index_k chars later. Since we're at start of the board, yes.

But `(?=.{index_a}[prnbqk])` similarly.

For sliding path, add lookaheads `(?=.{idx}\.)` for each intermediate square.

Full attack pattern:
`(?m)^(?=.{ik}K)(?=.{ia}[prnbqk])(?=.{m1}\.)(?=.{m2}\.)(?P<line>.*)\|1$`

This matches any line (tag 1) where the board has K at ik and black piece at ia with clear path. Replacement '' deletes the line. Wait `(?P<line>.*)\|1$` consumes everything including metadata. But `(?=.{ik}K)` is a lookahead at position 0; since `.*` consumes the line, the lookahead checks the original string before consumption, which is fine.

One concern: `(?=.{ia}[prnbqk])` will also match if the black piece at ia is any lowercase. We should restrict to the specific piece type for the geometry. For pawn attacks, we check 'p'. For knight, 'n'. For bishop/queen diagonal, 'b' or 'q'. For rook/queen orthogonal, 'r' or 'q'. For king, 'k'. We'll write separate patterns per piece type (or combine b|q). Fine.

Now, there's a subtlety: For the legality filter, we must consider that the board after a *capture* might have changed such that the attacker is captured (no longer present). The attack patterns check the resulting board, so that's correct.

Also, what about the moved king itself: after a king move, the white king is at the destination; attack detection checks if that square is attacked. Good. For moves that capture the attacker, the attacker is gone from the board, so no attack.

For pins: A pinned piece, if moved, leaves the king attacked along the pin line. The attack detection on the resulting board will catch it (because the sliding attacker now attacks the king through the vacated square). Good! So the legality filter handles pins automatically, as long as attack detection includes all sliding pieces with clear path.

For the king in check: if the current position has king in checkais, moves that fail to address it leave king attacked; filter catches. Moves that block/capture/king move result in no attack; they pass. Good.

So the legality filter is sufficient for all standard moves including pins and check evasion.

Now, what about castling through check and out of check? The legality filter only checks the *final* position (king on g1/c1). It doesn't check the intermediate f1/d1 or the starting e1. We'll handle castling specially as discussed.

Let's now think about en passant special legality: In en passant, the destination square (ep square) is behind the captured pawn; after the move, the black pawn is removed. The resulting board has white pawn on ep square and no black pawn adjacent. Attack detection on resulting board will catch if king exposed. So en passant legality handled.

But there is a special rule: en passant capture is illegal if it would expose the king to attack along the rank because removing the pawn opens a line? Wait that's exactly what the legality filter catches via sliding attack on resulting board. Good.

Now, metadata: After generating all legal moves, each line should be transformed back to FEN format:
`<compressed board> <side> <castle> <ep> <half> <full>`

Currently at the end we have tag-1 lines with internal format `BOARD|b|castle|ep|0|full|1`. We need to:
- Remove tag field and convert `|` separators to spaces.
- Compress dot runs in each rank to digits.

We can do this with regex pairs applied to all remaining lines:
1. Change tag `|1` at end to '' (remove last `|1`): pattern `\|1$` → ''.
2. Replace `|` with space: pattern `\|` → ' '. Now line becomes `<board> b <castle> <ep> 0 <full>`.
3. Compress each rank's dots: For each rank substring (8 chars between slashes or at ends), replace runs of dots with digit count. We need to compress each of the 8 rank groups separately, because counts are per rank. This is tricky with pure regex.

Compression: In a line like `rnb.k.nr b kq - 0 1` (after removing pipes), we need to transform each rank's 8-char section to FEN digit notation. The ranks are separated by '/'. For a rank like `rnb.k.nr`, the run `..`? Actually `rnb.k.nr` has one dot. For rank `P.P.....` → `P1P5` (P, 1 dot, P, 5 dots). We need to replace consecutive dots with the count.

How to compress with static regex replacements? Similar to expansion but reverse: replace sequences of dots with digits. But we must handle variable-length dot runs. We can do:
- Replace `........` with `8` (8 dots)
- Replace `.......` with `7`
- ...
- Replace `.` with `1`

If we process from longest run to shortest, after replacing 8-dot runs, the remaining dots could leave shorter runs. But careful: a rank like `P..P..` (2 dots,2 dots) would become `P2P2` if we first replace `..` with `2`. But if there's a run of 3 dots `...`, we should replace `...` with `3` first; otherwise after replacing `..` we'd get `2.` then... Let's order from 8 down to 1. Replace `........` first, then `.......`, etc. Because these are literal dot runs. After replacing all length-8 runs, length-7 runs, etc., we get digits. But there's a problem: a run of 8 dots could also be seen as two runs of 4, etc. If we process longest first, fine.

But the board string contains '/' separators; dot runs never cross '/', so counting within ranks is preserved. After compression, the board should have no dots, only pieces and digits.

However, there's an issue: The expanded board line has exactly 8 chars per rank and slashes; after compression, rank lengths vary (digits count for empty squares). That's the correct FEN.

But wait: our internal board used slashes between ranks but also the line has pipes `|` after the board. When we compress, we should only compress within the board part, not the metadata. If we've already replaced `|` with space, then the board part is before the first space. The compression patterns `........` etc. would also match any dots in metadata? Metadata has no dots except ep square like `-`, no dots. En passant square could be `-` or like `e3` (no dots). So no dots in metadata. But if ep is `-` there's no dots. Good. However there might be dots in castling field? No.

But there's a subtle issue: The compression patterns `........` with default re.sub (not multiline) will replace all occurrences globally. If applied to a line where board has rank with 8 dots, it becomes `8`. Goodmoi.

Now, there's an ordering problem with compression after replacing pipes: the line becomes `rnb.k.nr pp..pppp ... b kq - 0 1`? Wait after replacing `|` with space, the board slashes separate ranks but the ranks themselves are still expanded. Example internal board: `rnb.k.nr/pp..pppp/...B..../...` plus ` b kq - 0 1`. Compression patterns will replace dot runs with digits, giving `rnb1k1nr/p2p1ppp/3B4/...` which matches desired FEN. Good.

But hold on: after removing tag `|1` and replacing `|` with spaces, the line still has tag? We'll remove tag first.

Let's plan the final conversion:
- Pattern `(?m)\|1$` → `` (remove tag and pipe). Actually line ends with `|1`. After removal, we have `...|b|kq|-|0|1` (the tag gone, but still pipes). 
- Then `\|` → ` ` (space). Now `... b kq - 0 1`.
- Then compression patterns `........` → `8`, ..., `.` → `1` (applied globally). But careful: these patterns would also compress the dots in the *metadata* if any; there are none.
- Also we might need to clean up extra spaces? The FEN should have single spaces between fields: `<board> b <castle> <ep> <half> <full>`. Our conversion gives exactly that.

Wait, what about the initial internal line: `BOARD|w|kq|-|0|1|0`. After move generation, moved lines: `BOARD'|b|castle|ep|0|full|1`. At the end, we need `BOARD' b castle ep 0 full` (without the tag and with pipes→spaces). So the sequence `\|1$` → '' then `\|` → ' ' works. But the `\|1$` pattern removes the last `|1`. Then the remaining string has pipes between all fields including the one before the removed tag. After replacing `|` with spaces, we get spaces between all fields. Good.

However, there's a problem: after removing tag `|1`, the line still ends with `fullmove` and no tag; the `\|` → ' ' replacement will turn the remaining pipes into spaces. That yields `<board> b <castle> <ep> 0 <full>`. Good.

Now, empty lines cleanup: After legality filtering, illegal lines are replaced with '' (empty string), leaving empty lines (i.e., consecutive newlines). We need to remove them. Also the original tag-0 line(s) need removalousse.

Let's define a step to remove tag-0 lines: pattern `(?m)\|0$` → '' (replaces the whole line? Wait if pattern just matches the suffix `|0` at end, replacing with '' would leave the board and metadata but without tag, resulting in an incomplete internal line that might be malformed and then maybe matched by other patterns. Better to remove the entire line. We can pattern `(?m)^(?P<line>.*)\|0$` → ''. But `.*` is greedy; it will consume the entire line. Replacement '' deletes the line. This must be done after move generation.

Similarly, attack patterns delete tag-1 lines with `(?P<line>.*)\|1$` → ''.

After both, empty lines remain. Cleanup:
- `\n{2,}` → `\n` (collapse multiple newlines). But re.sub with `\n{2,}` on a string with `line1\n\nline2` will replace `\n\n` with `\n`, yielding `line1\nline2`. This works because `\n` is just two chars. We can use `\n+` → `\n` to collapse. But if the string starts with `\n` or ends with `\n` after deletions, we should trim:
  - `^\n` → '' (leading newline)
  - `\n$` → '' (trailing newline)
Order: collapse `\n+` to `\n`, then trim edges. But note: after collapsing, if there are leading/trailing newlines, trim. Use `^\n` and `\n$`.

However, there's a subtlety: If the output is empty (no legal moves), the split on '\n' of `""` gives `['']`, but there might be edge cases (e.g., checkmate/stalemate). The problem may not test such? "all possible legal next chess positions" — if none, the function should perhaps return empty list? Not specified. We can aim to return empty string → split gives ['']? Actually "".split('\n') = ['']. We might not be tested on positions with no legal moves, but for correctness maybe want empty list. We can add cleanup to result in empty string. Not critical.

Now, let's think about the move-generation patterns for each piece type in detail.

We'll generate patterns programmatically and write re.json. Let's outline the Python generator script.

We need to be careful about the exact line format and pattern construction.

Let's define helper functions.

Coordinate system:
- rows r=0..7 correspond to ranks 8..1. Files c=0..7 correspond to a..h.
- index in 71-char board: `idx = r*9 + c`.

Board representation in internal line: the board part is exactly 71 characters: for r in 0..7: for c in 0..7: char; if r<7 add '/'.

Actually, the internal board has slashes; index formula `r*9+c` works: r=0 positions 0-7, position 8 is '/', r=1 positions 9-16, etc.

Move patterns:
For a move from source S (rs,cs) to destination D (rd,cd) by white piece P:

Legal preconditions:
- Board[src] == P (white piece letter)
- Board[dest] in `[a-z.]` (empty or black piece) — not own white piece.
- For sliding moves, all intermediate squares (if any) are '.'.

We need to build the regex to match the whole internal line (with tag 0). We'll use the structure:
`(?m)^(?=...path lookaheads...)(?P<pre>.{dest_idx})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<mid>.{gap})(?P<src>P)(?P<suf>.*)\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)\|(?P<ep>[^|]*)\|(?P<half>[^|]*)\|(?P<full>[^|]*)\|(?P<tag>0)$`

Wait: there's an issue with using `(?P<pre>.{dest_idx})` when dest_idx < src_idx (dest before source). Let's derive both cases.

Case A: dest comes before source in string (dest_idx < src_idx). This corresponds to moves in the "upward" direction for white: toward rank8 (decreasing r) or leftward within a rank (decreasing c). For a generic pair:
The substring from start to dest inclusive: `pre` of length dest_idx, then dest char, then `mid` of length src_idx - dest_idx - 1, then source char P, then `suf` (rest of line).
Pattern: `^(?P<pre>.{dest_idx})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<mid>.{gap})(?P<src>P)(?P<suf>.*)\|...`
Replacement moved board: `\g<pre>P\g<mid>.\g<suf>` because dest becomes P, source becomes '.'.

Case B: source before dest (src_idx < dest_idx):
Pattern: `^(?P<pre>.{src_idx})(?P<src>P)(?P<mid>.{gap})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<suf>.*)\|...`
Replacement: `\g<pre>.\g<mid>P\g<suf>` (source becomes '.', dest becomes P).

For moves to same rank and left/right, these cases apply naturally.

For sliding path, we need to add lookaheads for intermediate squares. But careful: the lookaheads `(?=.{i}\.)` check from the start of the board (position 0). Since the match starts at `^` (start of line), and the board is exactly at the start, that's correct.

However, when we add metadata after board, the line begins with the board; `^` matches start of line, and lookaheads index into the board. Good.

Now, one problem: The `(?P<suf>.*)\|...` — `.*` is greedy and will match the rest of the line including metadata? Actually the pattern after suf is `\|(?P<castle>...)`. Since the board part has no '|', `.*` will stop at the first '|' in the line (after board). So `suf` holds the remainder of the board only. Wait but `.*` is greedy: it will first consume as much as possible, then backtrack to allow `\|` to match the first '|' after the board. Since the board part has no '|', the first '|' is exactly at position 71. So fine. But there's a nuance: `.*` can also match slashes and dots and letters, including uppercase. It will consume up to the last possible before backtracking to the first `|`. Because after the first `|` there are more `|` chars; greedy will consume to the end, then backtrack to the last `|`, but then the following pattern `\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)...` would need to match from there. Let's see: `.*` greedy would consume entire string including all metadata; then `\|` needs a literal pipe, so it backtracks to the last pipe (before tag), then `(?P<castle>[^|]*)` etc. But then `castle` would capture `full` etc incorrectly. This is a problem! The greedy `.*` followed by `\|(?P<castle>...)` could match at a later `|`, capturing wrong fields, and the whole match might still succeed with `suf` containing more than the board (including some metadata). We must prevent `suf` from crossing the first `|`.

Fix: Use `(?P<suf>[^|]*)` instead of `.*` to stop at the first pipe. Since board contains no pipe, `[^|]*` matches exactly the remainder of the board up to the first pipe. Great. So pattern should be:
`^(...)(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<mid>.{gap})(?P<src>P)(?P<suf>[^|]*)\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)\|(?P<ep>[^|]*)\|(?P<half>[^|]*)\|(?P<full>[^|]*)\|(?P<tag>0)$`

This ensures we capture exactly the board fields correctly.

Similarly, in attack detection patterns, `(?P<line>[^|]*\|...` hmm for deletion we just need `.*` to consume the whole line for replacement; that's fine since we're deleting. But lookaheads for attack patterns don't need captures.

Now, the replacement for move patterns must construct the new line with metadata. We'll write in Python:
`new_line = f"{pre}P{mid}.{suf}|b|{new_castle}|{new_ep}|0|{full}|1"` etc, using backreference strings in the JSON replacement. Since repl is a string, backrefs like `\g<pre>` are supported.

But note: the replacement also contains `\g<0>` for the original line. So `repl = r"\g<0>\n" + new_line_str`.

Now, generating all move patterns.

Let's define lists of black piece chars for destination: `[a-z.]` (lowercase or dot). We'll use regex char class `[a-z.]`.

White piece letters: `P`, `N`, `B`, `R`, `Q`, `K`.

For each white piece type, we'll generate move geometries.

### Pawn moves

White pawn moves from (r,c) to:
1. Push one: (r-1,c) if r>0 and dest in board; destination char in `[a-z.]`. No intermediate.
2. Push two: (r-2,c) if r==6 (rank2) and the intermediate (r-1,c) is '.'; destination empty `.` (must be '.' for double push); also dest char '.' (can't capture on double push). But our generic pattern allows dest `[a-z.]`; for double push, we should restrict dest to '.' specifically. We can use lookahead `(?=.{dest_idx}\.)` or pattern with `(?P<dst>\.)`. Simpler: use dest char group `\.` for double push. But we still need the general pattern structure; we can set `dst` to `\.` literal.
3. Captures: (r-1,c-1) and (r-1,c+1) if within board, and dest is black piece `[a-z]` (capture). Also en passant special: dest might be ep square.

For a capture, the destination must be a black piece (lowercase), not '.' (except en passant where dest is empty ep square). We'll generate normal captures with dest pattern `[a-z]` (lowercase). For pawn captures, if dest is empty, it's not a legal pawn move (except en passant). So dest char class should be `[a-z]`.

But wait: what about a pawn that captures en passant to an empty ep square? We'll handle separately.

For pawn push one and two: destination must be '.' (empty). Could be `\.` specifically.

For pawn moves, the source square must contain `P`.

Also, a white pawn on rank8 (r=0) cannot exist; promotions happen when reaching row0. Our move generator for a pawn on r=0? There should be no white pawn on rank8 except just-promoted, and we don't need to generate further moves from promoted positions. But if a white pawn is on rank7 (r=1) and pushes to rank8 (r=0), that's a promotion. We need to handle promotion to queen: the destination should be `Q` (not `P`), because promotion replaces the pawn with a queen. Also the pawn cannot push from rank7 to rank8 as a pawn; it becomes a queen. Since we only allow promotion to Queen, the move pattern for a pawn on r=1 moving to r=0 should place a `Q` at dest.

Similarly, captures that result in promotion (from r=1 to r=0 diagonal capture) also produce a queen.

So for pawn moves:
- If source row r == 1 (rank7), any push/capture to row 0 results in promotion. The destination piece should be 'Q', not 'P'. The source square becomes '.'.
- Also, if a pawn is already on row 0 (rank8) in the input? Not possible in a legal position (white pawns on rank8 would be promoted; FEN wouldn't have P on rank8). We can ignore.
- If source row r > 1, destination piece is 'P'.

We'll generate pawn patterns accordingly. Note: promotion to queen applies to both pushes and captures to the last rank.

Also, for double push, source must be on rank2 (r=6) and destination rank4 (r=4); intermediate r=5 must be empty. For a pawn on rank7 (r=1), double push? Not possible (double push only from rank2). So restrict r==6.

En passant: We'll handle separately.

Let's compute indices.

Push one: src=(r,c), dest=(r-1,c). dest_idx = (r-1)*9+c. If dest_idx < src_idx (since r-1<r), yes case A: dest before source. Pattern: `^(?=...)(?P<pre>.{dest_idx})(?P<dst>\.)(?P<mid>.{gap})(?P<src>P)(?P<suf>[^|]*)\|...` with gap = src_idx - dest_idx - 1 = (r*9+c) - ((r-1)*9+c) - 1 = 9 - 1 = 8? Wait difference = 9, gap = 8. Indeed there are 8 chars between dest and source (the rest of the two ranks + slash? Let's check: dest (r-1,c) to source (r,c) difference = 9, so 8 chars between). Good.

Replacement moved board: `\g<pre>P\g<mid>.\g<suf>` (dest becomes P, source '.').

Promotion push: dest piece 'Q': replacement `\g<pre>Q\g<mid>.\g<suf>`.

Push two: src=(6,c), dest=(4,c). dest_idx = 4*9+c, src_idx = 6*9+c, gap = 18-1=17. Intermediate square (5,c) index = 5*9+c. Need lookahead `(?=.{idx_mid}\.)`. Also dest must be '.': `(?P<dst>\.)`. Replacement dest piece 'P'. Ep new field should be set to the passed square: the ep target is the middle square (5,c), expressed as algebraic square. In FEN, ep is the square 'passed over', e.g., for white double push from rank2 to rank4, the ep square is the square on rank3 (row 5) — wait for white moving from rank2 (r=6) to rank4 (r=4), the passed-over square is rank3 (r=5). FEN ep format is a square like `e3`, which is rank3. Yes ep = (r=5, c) = rank3. We'll convert (r,c) to algebraic: file letter chr(97+c), rank number = 8-r. For r=5, rank=3. So ep square = file + str(8-5) = file+'3'. Good.

Captures: src=(r,c), dest=(r-1,c±1) within board spaces, dest char `[a-z]` (black piece). gap = |idx_dest - idx_src| - 1. Replacement dest 'P' or 'Q'.

En passant: source white pawn at (r=3,c) (rank5) captures ep square at (r=2, f) where f = c±1; black pawn at (r=3, f). Actually earlier: white pawn at (3, c), black pawn at (3, f), ep square (2, f), where f = c-1 or c+1. The capture: white pawn moves to (2, f), black pawn on (3,f) is removed. In board transformation:
- Source (3,c) becomes '.'.
- Destination (2,f) becomes 'P'.
- Black pawn square (3,f) becomes '.' (captured).
All other squares unchanged.

We need a pattern that handles three square modifications. This is more complex than simple source-dest replacement. We can write a pattern that matches the whole board with captures for the three squares. Since indices are known, we can construct a pattern:
For en passant to the left (dest file f = c-1): squares:
- black pawn at B = (r, f) where r=3, f=c-1. (The black pawn is on the same rank as white pawn, adjacent.)
- destination D = (r-1, f) = (2,f).
- source S = (r,c) = (3,c).

We need to identify the order of indices to build captures. This is messy because four changes (source, dest, captured pawn). But we can use a pattern with lookaheads to verify the squares, and then construct the modified board by capturing the board in segments between these indices.

Alternatively, maybe we can implement en passant by *two* substitutions per line: first, we could move the white pawn diagonally to the ep square (as a normal capture), then remove the black pawn. But our move-generation uses duplicate-and-tag; doing two substitutions would create intermediate lines that might be processed further or need tagging. Could be complex.

Let's implement en passant as a single pattern that modifies three squares. Using index captures:

We have three indices: S (source), D (dest), B (captured black pawn). Given they are distinct, sort them to create a pattern with segments:
Let `sorted_idx` = sorted([S,D,B]). Then board pattern capturing chars at each:
`^(?P<a>.{i0})(?P<ch0>.)(?P<b>.{i1-i0-1})(?P<ch1>.)(?P<c>.{i2-i1-1})(?P<ch2>.)(?P<d>[^|]*)\|...`

Then replacement constructs the modified board by replacing these three chars with their new values (D->P, S->'.', B->'.').

But the three chars may not be in a convenient order (e.g., D and B have same rank adjacent, S adjacent). We can determine for each en passant move the order and build replacement string accordingly.

Since we generate patterns programmatically, we can compute for each ep move:
- idx_source (white pawn)
- idx_dest (ep square)
- idx_captured (black pawn)
- new_chars: at dest -> 'P'; at source -> '.'; at captured -> '.'.

Then build pattern:
```
^(?P<a>.{i0})(?P<ch0>.)(?P<b>.{i1-i0-1})(?P<ch1>.)(?P<c>.{i2-i1-1})(?P<ch2>.)(?P<d>[^|]*)\|...
```
with lookaheads:
- `(?=.{idx_source}P)` source is P
- `(?=.{idx_dest}\.)` dest is empty (should be)
- `(?=.{idx_captured}p)` captured is black pawn (lowercase p)
- Also the FEN ep field must equal the ep square string. We can add to the pattern `\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)\|(?P<ep>c6)\|...` requiring ep field exact.

Wait, the en passant move is only legal if the FEN ep field equals the specific square. So the pattern must include `\|(?P<ep>...)\|` matching the exact ep square. In our line, ep field is between two pipes. We can include literal ep square in the pattern: `\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)\|c6\|`? But we also need to capture castle for reuse. We can write `\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)\|(?P<ep>c6)\|...`. That matches only when ep is c6.

Then replacement for the new line: modified board with three changes, color b, castle unchanged? Actually en passant doesn't affect castling rights (unless it captures a rook? en passant captures a pawn, not a rook; castling rights unaffected). So castle stays same. Ep becomes '-'. So new line: `<modified_board>|b|<castle>|-|0|<full>|1`.

The replaced board: We need to construct from captures `<a><ch0'><b><ch1'><c><ch2'><d>` where ch0'/ch1'/ch2' are the three new chars in sorted-index order.

We can compute in Python during generation.

Also, we need to check that the black pawn is indeed on the captured square (lookahead `(?=.{idx_captured}p)`). And the white pawn is P at source.

Great. We'll generate en passant patterns for both sides (left/right) for each file f (ep square file) and each possible white pawn file c = f-1 or f+1, provided source white pawn is on rank5 (r=3) and black pawn on rank5 (r=3), dest on rank6 (r=2). Wait in our row indexing rank5 r=3, rank6 r=2. The white pawn captures from rank5 to rank6 (r=3 -> r=2). But earlier we said white pawn on rank5, black pawn on rank5. However from sample? In the sample, the black king etc; no ep needed. En passant in general for white to move: Black just moved from rank7 to rank5, so black pawn on rank5 (r=3). White pawn on rank5 (r=3) adjacent. Yes. So pattern in row coordinates: white pawn at (3,c), black pawn at (3,f), dest (2,f). Good.

But wait: Is the black pawn on rank5 (r=3) after its double push from rank7 (r=1) to rank5 (r=3)? Yes. The white pawn that can capture en passant is on rank5 (r=3) as well. Correct.

So en passant patterns: for each ep file f (0..7), for each side dir in [-1,+1]:
- white pawn source file c = f + dir (i.e., if capturing from right? Let's define: if white pawn is to the left of ep square (c = f-1), it captures to the right, dest file=f. If white pawn is to the right (c = f+1), captures to the left, dest file=f.
- Check source c within 0..7, and source rank 3, dest rank 2, black pawn at (3,f).
- Source must be within board. Good.

But there's a catch: the white pawn on rank5 (r=3) and black pawn on rank5 (r=3) — that means the black pawn just double-pushed to rank5, and the white pawn is on the same rank. That's correct for en passant.

Now, what about the "ep square" in FEN for white to move: it's always on rank6 (r=2)? Yes, black double push from rank7 to rank5 passes over rank6, so ep square is on rank6. Good.

Now, castling.

Castling moves: kingside and queenside.

Kingside (O-O):
- Source king at e1 (r=7,c=4), dest g1 (r=7,c=6). Rook h1 (r=7,c=7) moves to f1 (r=7,c=5).
- Squares f1 (r=7,c=5) and g1 (r=7,c=6) must be empty.
- Castling right 'K' must be present in castle field.
- King not in check at start, and f1/g1 not attacked.

Pattern matching: We need to match board with K at e1, R at h1, f1/g1 empty. Also castle field contains 'K'. We also need to transform four squares: e1 K -> '.', h1 R -> '.', f1 '.' -> 'R', g1 '.' -> 'K'. Plus metadata: color b, castle rights remove 'K' and 'Q' (since king moved; actually king moves so both white rights removed), ep '-', tag 1.

Wait queenside removes both too (king moves). Yes any castling removes both white castling rights because the king moves. In FEN, after white castles, white loses both K and Q. So new castle = original with K and Q removed (i.e., only black's kq remain, if any). So for white castling, we can simply remove uppercase K and Q from castle field.

For black rights: castling doesn't affect black's rights.

Similarly, queenside: king e1 -> c1, rook a1 -> d1; squares b1,c1,d1 must be empty; right 'Q' present; e1/c1/d1/b1 not attacked.

Pattern construction for castling is complex due to four-square modificationUTE. We'll use the sorted-index approach like en passant.

Let's define for kingside:
- idx_K = e1 = 7*9+4 = 67
- idx_R = h1 = 7*9+7 = 70
- idx_f1 = 7*9+5 = 68
- idx_g1 = 7*9+6 = 69
Sorted order: 67(K),68(f1),69(g1),70(R) — consecutive! Nice. The pattern:
`^(?=...lookaheads...)(?P<a>.{67})(?P<ch0>.)(?P<b>.{0})(?P<ch1>.)(?P<c>.{0})(?P<ch2>.)(?P<d>.{0})(?P<ch3>.)(?P<e>[^|]*)\|...`
Actually with consecutive indices, after ch0 at 67, b is empty (0 chars), ch1 at 68, c empty, ch2 at 69, d empty, ch3 at 70, then rest.

We need lookaheads:
- `(?=.{67}K)` king
- `(?=.{70}R)` rook
- `(?=.{68}\.)` f1 empty
- `(?=.{69}\.)` g1 empty
- castle field contains 'K': in metadata capture, we can require the castle group to match `[^|]*K[^|]*` so that it contains K. E.g., `\|(?P<castle>[^|]*K[^|]*)\|`.

Also need "not attacked" conditions for e1,f1,g1. We'll add negative lookaheads (discussed later).

Replacement: new chars at sorted indices: 67 becomes '.' (king leaves), 68 becomes 'R' (rook to f1), 69 becomes 'K' (king to g1), 70 becomes '.' (rook leaves).
New board = `\g<a>.\g<b>R\g<c>K\g<d>.\g<e>` (since b,c,d are empty captures between).
Then metadata: `|b|` + updated castle + `|-|0|<full>|1`.

Queenside:
- idx_e1 = 67 (K)
- idx_d1 = 7*9+3 = 66
- idx_c1 = 65
- idx_b1 = 64
- idx_a1 = 7*9+0 = 63
Sorted: a1(63), b1(64), c1(65), d1(66), e1(67), consecutive! Good.
Lookaheads:
- K at e1 (67)
- R at a1 (63)
- b1 (64) '.', c1 (65) '.', d1 (66) '.'
- castle contains 'Q'.
New squares: a1 63 -> '.' (rook leaves), d1 66 -> 'R' (rook to d1), c1 65 -> 'K' (king to c1), b1 64 remains '.' (empty).
So new chars: 63 '.', 64 '.', 65 'K', 66 'R', 67 '.'.
Board replacement accordingly.

The "not attacked" conditions for castling: e1, f1, g1 (kingside) or e1, d1, c1 (queenside) must not be attacked by black. Also typically the king's path squares (f1 for kingside, d1 for queenside) and destination (g1/c1) must not be attacked, and e1 (starting) not attacked.

We can add negative lookaheads to the castling pattern that assert no black piece attacks those squares. Since the castling pattern already matches the board, we can prepend `(?!<attack_pattern_on_square>)` for each square, where `<attack_pattern_on_square>` is a regex that would match the line if that square is attacked.

But defining "attack on square X" as a regex matching the whole line is itself a set of patterns. We could embed an alternation of all attack geometries on X. This is essentially the legality check for that square in the *current* board. Since we already generate legality attack patterns for the king, we can reuse the same builder to create "attack on square X" patterns. For each square X and each potential black attacker geometry, we can create a regex fragment that matches the line if X is attacked. Then for castling, we embed `(?!(?:fragment1|fragment2|...))` for X=e1,f1,g1.

But these fragments are themselves full-line patterns anchored with `^`. Inside a negative lookahead at the start of the castling pattern, we can use `(?!(?:...))` but the inner fragments need to match from the current position (start of line). We can write them as `(?!(?:pattern1|pattern2|...))` where each pattern matches the line content. Since each fragment is a full pattern like `(?=.{ik}K)(?=.{ia}[prnbqk])...` — wait attack on square X means a black piece attacks X (not necessarily a white king there). For castling safety, X may not contain a white king (f1/g1 are empty), so we can't require a K at X. Instead, "square X is attacked" means there is a black piece whose attack line passes through X. We can detect this by combining the attacker presence and path clear to X. The square X itself may be empty (or occupied by K for e1). So attack-on-square patterns are separate from king-attack patterns.

We need to detect attacks on arbitrary empty squares for castling. This is more general than king attack detection. But we can generate similar patterns: for each square X and each black attacker geometry, if the attacker's line passes through X with clear path, then X is attacked. For pawns, a black pawn attacks X if X is at (rp+1, cp±1). For knights etc.

This is essentially generating all black attacks on a given square. We can generate these patterns programmatically and embed as negative lookaheads.

- King: black king adjacent (rX±1, cX±1 etc within 1). Check `(?=.{ia}k)`.

For attack fragments, the "path" for sliding is between attacker and X; we need lookaheads for each intermediate square being '.'.

Actually we can just handle castle updates with a few regex passes:
1. Remove 'K' and 'Q' from castle on lines tagged with `K` (king moved) or `C` (castling). Since these tags mark king moves.

Let's refine the line tag scheme. Move patterns will set tag to one of a set of codes:
- `N` normal move (no castle update)
- `K` king moved (remove K,Q)
- `H` rook from h1 moved (remove K)
- `A` rook from a1 moved (remove Q)
- `h` capture of black rook on h8 (remove k)
- `a` capture of black rook on a8 (remove q)
- `C` kingside castling / `c` queenside castling (remove K,Q for white; castling also implies king move so tag K-like)
- `D` double pawn push (normal castle-wise, but also wron move) — castle update none, ep set.
- `E` en passant (normal castle update none)

Then later, a pattern for each effect can match if the tag contains the relevant letter.

Let's define effect markers as single characters appended to a tag string:
- `M` = normal (no castle changes)

This works! We don't need to enumerate all castle values. Just two patterns for each effect (or reuse with char class).

But careful: the tag-0 removal pattern and attack patterns both replace with ''; consecutive newlines will remain; final cleanup handles.

Now, final conversion must also handle the tag field. After attack filtering and metadata updates, lines still have tag. We'll convert as described.

Let's think about a subtlety with `re.sub` and patterns with `(?m)^...$` when the string contains multiple lines: The pattern `(?m)^[^\n]*\|0$` will match the entire content of each tag-0 line (since `[^\n]*` up to newline). Replacing with '' removes the line content. Good.

Now, let's double-check the move pattern construction with the duplicate mechanism.

Pattern for a normal move, e.g., knight from d4 to f3:
- src (4,3) index = 4*9+3=39? Wait rank4 r=4? Let's define row r=0=rank8. Knight on d4 => rank4 => r=4? Actually rank4 has r=4 (since rank8 r=0, rank4 r=4). Yes. c=3 (d). idx = 4*9+3 = 39. dest f3 => rank3 r=5, file f c=5. idx = 5*9+5 = 50. dest after source.
Pattern: `(?m)^(?P<pre>.{39})(?P<src>N)(?P<mid>.{10})(?P<dst>[a-z.])(?P<suf>[^|]*)\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)\|(?P<ep>[^|]*)\|(?P<half>[^|]*)\|(?P<full>[^|]*)\|(?P<tag>0)$`
Wait mid gap = 50-39-1 = 10. The mid contains irrelevant squares (e4-h4, a3-c3? etc), which is fine for knight (no path).
Lookaheads: none (knight jumps).
Replacement: original + newline + `\g<pre>.\g<mid>N\g<suf>|b|\g<castle>|-|0|\g<full>|M`

But wait: the `dst` char is consumed and replaced by N; source '.' added; mid unchanged. Yes.

Now, the tag in the pattern for a knight move is `M` (normal). But the pattern's match requires tag `0` (original). Replacement's new line has tag `M`. Good.

However, there's a problem: The move pattern matches the original line and produces a moved line with tag `M`. But the moved line's tag `M` is not `0`, so future move patterns won't match it (since they require tag `0`). Good.

Now, what about a source piece that is a white piece moving to a destination occupied by a black piece? dest char `[a-z.]`; capture is allowed. Good.

But what about a piece moving to a square occupied by another white piece (uppercase)? dest char class `[a-z.]` excludes uppercase, so no match. Good.

Now, let's think about pawn move patterns with promotion (r=1 to r=0). Destination piece becomes Q. The tag for promotion move should be normal (no castle change). Also, the halfmove clock can be 0 regardless; fine.

Now, castling patterns with `\g<0>` duplication: Same mechanism.

Now, the legality filter attack detection. Let's detail generation.

For each king square K (rk,ck), enumerate attackers:

1. Black pawns:
   A black pawn at (rk-1, ck-1) or (rk-1, ck+1) attacks the king at (rk,ck)? Let's verify direction: Earlier established black pawn at (r,c) attacks (r+1,c-1) and (r+1,c+1). So if king at K, a black pawn at (rk-1, ck-1) or (rk-1, ck+1) attacks it. So attackers at (rk-1, ck∓? if ck-1 then ca=ck-1; if ck+1 then ca=ck+1). Yes.
   Path: none.
   Pattern checks `p` at attacker index and `K` at king index.

2. Black knights:
   Knight at (rk+dr, ck+dc) where (dr,dc) in [(-2,-1),(-2,1),(-1,-2),(-1,2),(1,-2),(1,2),(2,-1),(2,1)].
   Check `n` at attacker.

3. Black bishop/queen diagonals:
   For each diagonal direction (dr,dc) in [(1,1),(1,-1),(-1,1),(-1,-1)], for distance d=1..7 while within board:
   attacker at (rk+d*dr, ck+d*dc). Path squares between attacker and king: (rk + i*dr, ck + i*dc) for i=1..d-1. Need all '.'.
   Check attacker char `[bq]`.
4. Black rook/queen orthogonals:
   For each orthogonal direction (dr,dc) in [(1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)], distance d:
   attacker at (rk+d*dr, ck+d*dc). Path squares between.
   Check attacker char `[rq]`.

5. Black king:
   Adjacent squares (rk±1,ck±1) distance 1. Check `k`. (Note: white king moving adjacent to black king would be illegal because it's moving into attack; the attack detection catches it.)

For each attacker geometry, we create a pattern:
`(?m)^(?=.{idx_k}K)(?=.{idx_a}[piece_class])(?=path...)[^\n]*\|[^0]...$`? Actually after tag-0 removal, we can just match whole line `(?m)^(?=...)[^\n]*$` replacement ''. But we need to ensure the line ends with a tag (not original). Since we remove tag-0 first, any remaining line is a moved line. So attack pattern can be `(?m)^(?=...)[^\n]*$` → ''. This deletes any non-empty line that has the attack. But wait, `[^\n]*$` requires the line to contain something; empty lines (after previous deletions) won't match because `[^\n]*` can match zero chars and `$` matches, so it *will* match empty lines! If we run attack patterns after some lines were deleted leaving empty lines, the attack pattern `(?m)^(?=...)[^\n]*$` would match empty lines too (since lookaheads might succeed vacuously? Lookaheads like `(?=.{idx}K)` won't succeed on an empty line because there aren't enough chars; so no match). So empty lines won't be deleted by attack patterns. Good.

But to be safe, we should delete tag-0 lines first; the remaining lines are non-empty moved lines with tags.

Attack pattern with lookaheads at start: `(?m)^(?=.{ik}K)(?=.{ia}[a-z])(?=.{m1}\.)...[^\n]*$` replacement ''.

Important: `(?=.{ik}K)` with `{ik}` where ik could be up to 70; works.

Now, one concern: In the 71-char board with slashes, the index of a square includes slashes. The lookaheads `(?=.{id}K)` check the char at that linear index, which corresponds to the square. Good.

Now, the metadata updates.

After attack filtering, we still have lines with tag strings. We need to remove tag-0? already done. Now apply castle-removal patterns for markers. Also, after removing tag-0, the remaining lines have tags like `M`, `K`, `H`, etc. The castle field currently is the original castling rights (since move patterns didn't modify it). We now remove letters based on tags.

Patterns for removal (as designed above). We'll generate for each (marker_char, castle_char_to_remove) pair:
- ('K','K'), ('K','Q'): because king moved.
- ('H','K'): rook h1 moved.
- ('A','Q'): rook a1 moved.
- ('h','k'): captured h8 rook.
- ('a','q'): captured a8 rook.

Also, for castling lines, tag should include 'K' so K and Q removed. So castling move patterns set tag='K' (plus maybe 'C' for castling if needed, but 'K' suffices).

Wait, what about a white king moving from e1: tag='K' removes K and Q. Correct.

What about a white rook moving from h1: tag='H' removes K. Correct. But what if the rook from h1 moves to h8 capturing black rook? Tag='Hh'; patterns remove K and k. Good.

Now, one more subtlety: A rook from a1 moving to a8 capturing black rook: tag='Aa'; removes Q and q. Good.

What about a rook from h1 moving (tag H) and also a capture of h8? In one move impossible (h1 to h8 is a vertical move capturing h8 rook; yes tag 'Hh').

Now, after these removal patterns, the castle field may have multiple letters removed. If after removal it becomes empty (e.g., castle was 'KQ' and king moves), the field becomes an empty string between pipes: `||`. We need to convert empty castle to `-`. Similarly, if the original castle was '-' (no rights), removal patterns won't match because no letter to remove; castle stays '-'? Wait '-' is a hyphen, not K/Q. Our move patterns set castle field? They leave the original castle (could be '-'). The removal patterns only remove letters, leaving '-' unchanged. Good.

But if castle was e.g., 'K' and king moves, pattern removing K will change 'K' to '' (empty). We need to ensure empty castle is represented as '-' in final output. FEN uses '-' for "no castling rights". So after removal patterns, if the castle field is empty, we need to replace it with '-'. We can add a pattern: `\|(?P<castle>)\|` → `|-|`? Specifically, match `\|\|` where that's an empty castle field (between color and ep), and replace with `|-|`. But careful, there are other empty fields? EP could be '-' (not empty). Half/full not empty. So an empty castle occurs as `|b||-|...` after color b. We can add pattern `\|b\|\|` → `\|b\|-\|`. Let's do that.

Also, in final conversion we replace `|` with space; an empty castle would create double space, which is wrong. So convert empty castle to '-'.

Now, what about the EP field? Move patterns set EP literally to '-' or a square. So EP field is always non-empty. Good.

Now, let's think about whether the "color" field is always 'b' in moved lines. Yes, move patterns set color to 'b'.

Now, final conversion:
1. Remove tag field: `(?m)\|[^|]*(?:\|0)?$`? Hmm, we need to remove the last `|TAG` from each line. Since tag is the last field, pattern `\|[^|]*$` would also match the fullmove field (the last before tag). Wait line: `...|b|castle|ep|0|full|TAG`. There are 7 fields. The last pipe separates full and TAG. We want to remove `|TAG`. Pattern: `\|(?P<tag>[^|]*)$` → ''. This removes the final `|TAG`. But careful, this pattern would match every line's last field; after tag-0 lines removed)Skip. Fine.

Actually we need to keep the fullmove field? The final FEN requires both halfmove and fullmove fields (though allowed wrong). Our line after removing tag: `BOARD|b|castle|ep|0|full`. That has six fields separated by pipes. Good. Then replace all remaining `|` with spaces: `\|` → ' '. Yields `BOARD b castle ep 0 full`.

But wait, after removing tag, the string ends with `...|full`; no trailing pipe. Replacing `|` with space gives `BOARD b castle ep 0 full`. Good.

2. Replace `\|` with space.
3. Compress dots (as above).
4. Cleanup newlines/empty lines.

But there's a catch: When we replace `|` with space, we must not replace any `|` in the board (there are none). Good.

Now, the compression of dot runs: The board part after replacing pipes with spaces is the first field. Dot runs appear only there (and possibly in ep? ep is a square like 'e3', no dots). Good.

Let's verify with sample mentally.

Now, let's think about potential bugs with the duplicate-and-tag and the order of move patterns.

The move patterns are applied sequentially. Each pattern matches tag-0 lines and adds a new tag-nonzero line. After all move patterns, we have original (tag0) plus all pseudo-legal moves (tag tags). Then we remove tag0. Attack patterns remove illegal. Good.

But one issue: The attack patterns use `[^\n]*$` to match whole lines. After we remove tag0 lines, the remaining lines have tags. Attack patterns match if the board has K attacked. But they will match *all* such lines including those where the attack is happening on a *black* king? We check `K` (white king) specifically, so only white king attacks. Good.

Now, there's another subtlety: The attack detection checks if the white king is attacked *in the resulting board*. For a move that vacates a square and leaves the white king on some square, if the king is now attacked, the attack patterns delete the line. This correctly removes moves that leave the king in check.

But we must ensure the attack detection considers *all* black pieces, including the black king. We'll include.

Now, there's a subtle issue with pins and the king: Suppose a pinned white piece moves, exposing the king to a sliding attack. Attack detection on the resulting board will detect the attack through the vacated square. Good.

But consider an en passant capture that opens a rank line (e.g., removing a pawn between a rook and the king). Since we remove the black pawn and move the white pawn, the board changes; attack detection sees the resulting board and catches any newly opened attackagonist. Good.

Now, let's think about whether the attack detection might be fooled by the *moved piece* blocking an attack. That's exactly how legal moves work; if a captured/moved piece blocked an attack, removing it might expose the king, which we detect. Good.

Now, let's consider "check" from the black king adjacency: A white move that moves the white king adjacent to the black king is illegal because the white king would be in check by the black king. Attack detection includes black king adjacency, so it removes it. Also, a white non-king move that blocks a black king attack etc. Fine.

Now, there's one more complex legality: A white move that *discovers* a check from a black pawn due to moving a piece that was blocking a pawn's diagonal attack? Pawns attack adjacent diagonals; a piece could block a pawn's attack on the king. If you move that blocker, the pawn attacks the king. Attack detection on resulting board catches it. Good.

Now, let's consider the castling "not through check" for queenside: we check e1 and d1 not attacked in current board. Actually for queenside, the king passes through d1 (r=7,c=3) and lands on c1 (r=7,c=2); squares b1,c1,d1 must be empty, and e1,d1,c1 not attacked. We'll check e1 and d1 with attack fragments; c1 is final (covered by legality filter). Actually should also check b1? The king doesn't pass through b1 (it goes e1->d1->c1); b1 is between rook a1 and d1, but the king doesn't occupy b1. The rule: squares the king crosses (d1) and destination (c1) and starting (e1) must not be attacked; b1 is not crossed by the king but the rook crosses it? Actually in queenside castling, the rook moves from a1 to d1, passing through b1 and c1? The rook moves over b1,c1 to d1. The rule only cares about king's path e1,d1,c1, not rook's path (except b1,c1,d1 must be empty). So checking e1 and d1 is sufficient; final filter covers c1. Good.

For kingside, rook moves h1 to f1 passing over g1; king path e1->f1->g1. Check e1,f1; g1 final filter.

Now, the attack fragments for e1/d1 should consider attacks from black pieces on the current board. We'll generate them.

Let's write a brute-force generator in Python that:
- Converts input FEN to internal line.
- Generates pseudo-legal moves (white) using our (source,dest) patterns as pairs.
- Runs attack filtering and metadata updates.

Actually, we can initially implement the move-generation logic in plain Python to test concepts, then translate to regex pairs.

But the deliverable must be regex-based. Let's just write the generator script that builds regex pairs, then run the sample and check.py to iterate.

Let's design the generator script structure.

We'll need a function to build a move pattern given:
- piece char P (white uppercase)
- source (rs,cs), dest (rd,cd)
- dest_regex: `[a-z.]` or `\.` or `[a-z]`
- path squares list (for sliding)
- extra lookaheads (e.g., for double push intermediate)
- metadata: tag string (markers)
- ep_new: '-' or square
- replacement piece char (for promotion = 'Q' else same piece)
- moving to dest: whether dest before source.

Function to make the pattern and replacement.

Let's write the generator to produce a list of [pattern,repl] pairs.

First, internal format helpers:

```python
def idx(r,c): return r*9+c

def square_to_str(r,c): return chr(97+c)+str(8-r)
```

Move pattern builder:

```python
def make_move_pair(P, src, dst, dest_re='[a-z.]', path=[], look=[],
                   tag='M', ep='-', prom=False, cast_effect=[]):
    rs,cs = src; rd,cd = dst
    isrc = idx(rs,cs); idst = idx(rd,cd)
    piece = 'Q' if prom else P
    # path and look are lists of indices that must be '.'
    pre_look = ''.join(f'(?=.{{{i}}}\\.)' for i in path+look)
    if idst < isrc:
        gap = isrc - idst - 1
        pat = (f"(?m)^{pre_look}(?P<pre>.{{{idst}}})(?P<dst>{dest_re})"
               f"(?P<mid>.{{{gap}}})(?P<src>{P})(?P<suf>[^|]*)\\|"
               f"(?P<castle>[^|]*)\\|(?P<ep>[^|]*)\\|(?P<half>[^|]*)\\|"
               f"(?P<full>[^|]*)\\|(?P<tag>0)$")
        newboard = f"\\g<pre>{piece}\\g<mid>.\\g<suf>"
    else:
        gap = idst - isrc - 1
        pat = (f"(?m)^{pre_look}(?P<pre>.{{{isrc}}})(?P<src>{P})"
               f"(?P<mid>.{{{gap}}})(?P<dst>{dest_re})(?P<suf>[^|]*)\\|"
               f"(?P<castle>[^|]*)\\|(?P<ep>[^|]*)\\|(?P<half>[^|]*)\\|"
               f"(?P<full>[^|]*)\\|(?P<tag>0)$")
        newboard = f"\\g<pre>.\\g<mid>{piece}\\g<suf>"
    repl = f"\\g<0>\n{newboard}|b|\\g<castle>|{ep}|0|\\g<full>|{tag}"
    return (pat, repl)
```

This assumes line format `BOARD|w|castle|ep|half|full|0`. Wait in the pattern we don't capture color field; we just skip from board to castle. The board `suf` stops at first `|`, then the next literal `\|` matches. But the color field is between board and castle! Oops, our line format is `BOARD|w|castle|ep|half|full|tag`. In the pattern after `suf[^|]*` we put `\|` then `(?P<castle>...)`. But actually after the board comes `|w|` — the color field! We captured `suf` as the rest of the board (up to first `|`), then `\|` matches the pipe after board. Then the next field is `w`, then `|castle`. But our pattern's `(?P<castle>[^|]*)` immediately after the first `\|` would capture `w` (color), not castle! That's wrong.

We need to account for the color field. The line after board is `|w|castle|ep|half|full|tag`. So pattern should be:
`... (?P<suf>[^|]*)\|(?P<color>w)\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)\|(?P<ep>[^|]*)\|(?P<half>[^|]*)\|(?P<full>[^|]*)\|(?P<tag>0)$`

And replacement: `... newboard|b|...` (change color to b). We don't need the color capture in replacement except to set to b. So update pattern builder accordingly.

Pattern tail: `\|(?P<color>w)\|(?P<castle>[^|]*)\|(?P<ep>[^|]*)\|(?P<half>[^|]*)\|(?P<full>[^|]*)\|(?P<tag>0)$`
Replacement: `\g<0>\n{newboard}|b|\g<castle>|{ep}|0|\g<full>|{tag}`

So compute tag:
```

    tag_chars.append('K')
if P == 'R':

    if src == (7,0): tag_chars.append('A')

    tag_chars.append('h' if dest==(0,7) else 'a')
if not tag_chars: tag='M'
else: tag=''.join(tag_chars)
```

Now, the negative attack fragments: Need a function `build_attack_fragments_for_square(rk,ck)` that returns a list of regex fragments each matching the whole line if the square (rk,ck) is attacked by black. Then combine as alternation: `(?:frag1|frag2|...)`. We'll embed `(?!(?:...))`.

But there's a subtlety: The fragments inside `(?!...)` will be evaluated starting at the position of the negative lookahead, which is at the start of the line (since the castling pattern starts with `^`). The fragments we build should match from that position (start of line). They will use `(?=.{idx_attacker}...)` lookaheads. Good.

Let's implement `square_attacked_fragments(rk,ck)`:

We'll create fragments as strings: `(?=.{ia}[class])(?=path...).*` where `.*` consumes the rest (so the fragment matches the whole line from start). But wait: `(?=.{ia}[class])` is a zero-width assertion at position 0. Following it with `.*` consumes the line (with default dot doesn't match newline, but the line has no newline; `.*` matches to end). The match succeeds if the lookahead passes. Since we're inside `(?!...)`, if the fragment matches, the negative lookahead fails.

One issue: Inside `(?!...)`, the regex engine may try the alternation; if a fragment matches, the overall `(?!...)` fails. Good.

Let's write `def square_attack_fragments(rk,ck)` returning list of strings.

- Append `|0`.

Wait after replacing `#` with `|`, the string is `BOARD|w kq - 0 1`. Then replace spaces (all of them) with `|`: `BOARD|w|kq|-|0|1`. Good.

But hold on: The lookup `(?=[^#]*#)` expansion patterns must be applied BEFORE we replace `#` with `|`. Yes.

Also, note that the FEN may have `w` as the side; using ` w ` pattern might be brittle if there are two spaces? FEN standard uses single spaces. Good.

But the initial side could be `w` (given we only see white to move). So pattern to replace first space: ` (?=w )` replaces the space before 'w' with '#'. Actually ` (?=w )` matches a space followed by `w `; this is the space before `w`. It will correctly match the first space (since board has no spaces). Good.

Edge: If the FEN has castling '-' and ep '-', etc., fine.

Now, the digit expansion patterns with lookahead `(?=[^#]*#)` might have interactions: if we replace digit `1` with `.`, the lookahead `[^#]*#` still holds for subsequent digits? The `.` doesn't contain #, so the lookahead remains valid. But note: after replacing a `1` with `.`, the string length changes (1 char to 1 dot? `1`→`.` same length, `2`→`..` length 2, etc.). The lookahead `[^#]*#` just needs *some* # ahead; it doesn't depend on length. So fine.

But there's a subtlety: The pattern `(?=[^#]*#)` for digit `1` will match every `1` in the board. If the board has `11` (two consecutive empty squares), each `1` matches and is replaced by `.`, giving `..`. Good.

Now, the metadata digits (half/full) are after #, so not matched. EP digit after # not matched. 

Now, once digits are expanded and # replaced, we have internal line.

Let's verify with sample: Input `"rnb1k1nr/p2p1ppp/3B4/1p1NPN1P/6P1/3P1Q2/P1P5/q4Kb1 w kq - 0 1"`.

Step 1: Replace space before w with #: `rnb1k1nr/p2p1ppp/3B4/1p1NPN1P/6P1/3P1Q2/P1P5/q4Kb1#w kq - 0 1`.

Step 2: Expand digits before #. Let's expand each digit. The pattern `(?P<d>1)(?=[^#]*#)` etc. But careful: the lookahead `[^#]*#` means from after the digit to #, only non-# chars. In the board, slashes are non-#, so okay. In `rnb1k1nr`, after first `1` is `k1nr/p... # w...`; there is a # eventually. So matches. Good.

After expansion: `rnb.k.nr/p..p.ppp...` wait let's not manually.

Then step 3: replace # with `|`: `...|w kq - 0 1`.
Step 4: replace spaces with `|`: `...|w|kq|-|0|1`.
Step 5: append `|0`: `...|w|kq|-|0|1|0`.

Good.

Now, the internal line's board part must be exactly 71 chars. Let's verify expansion counts: each rank had 8 squares; after expansion with dots, yes.

Potential issue: The input FEN could have `8` digits etc. We have patterns for 1-8. Good.

Let's write a Python script `/app/generate_re.py` that constructs the list and writes /app/re.jsonfuneme. We'll run it.

We need to be careful with regex string escaping in JSON. The `repl` strings contain backslashes like `\g<0>` which in JSON must be `\\g<0>`. When we write the Python list and json.dump, it will escape automatically. Good.

Also, patterns contain `\g` etc? Patterns use `(?P<name>...)`, lookaheads `(?=...)`, literal `\.` etc. In Python source, we'll write raw strings.
